Directly under the crown, five equilateral triangles hang point-down, one beneath each outer side of the crown. Around the lower wall stand five more five-pointed stars, evenly spaced, each centred a little below halfway down the wall on a point where five struts meet; the lowest triangle of each lower star rests its wide side on the base ring with its point up at the star's centre. Between each pair of neighbouring lower stars, one of five more equilateral triangles stands point-up on the base ring. So the whole dome is six stars of five triangles plus ten equilateral triangles, forty in all. Every other junction has six struts meeting (ten of them); the ten corners of the base ring have four. (THREE) ONLY TWO STRUT LENGTHS: the SHORT struts (thirty lines) are exactly the spokes of the six stars -- every spoke is short and nothing else is. The LONG struts (thirty-five lines) are everything else: the outline of each star, the sides of the equilateral triangles, and all ten sides of the base ring. Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. THIS FIT-OUT: the home seed, standing as a plain hemisphere, with six window panels, two skylight panels, one door panel, two opening windows, one flue panel, and plain closed panels in the other bays. SCENE: the dome at the edge of a brick-built woodland clearing at dusk, warm light in the windows, a small stove pipe leaving one upper bay. The frame is the same stem cell as every other image; only the panels and modules differ. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
```

**Overlay:** HOME SEED -- A starter domicile: wash, cook, drain and see out. The camera ring sits on the seal cap and the screen inside shows what it sees.

There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. THIS FIT-OUT: the bunker seed, buried, with plain closed panels in every bay but the door. SCENE: the dome buried in a green studded hillside so only a low mound shows, the apex riser breaking the turf and a stair descending to a door set in the slope. The frame is the same stem cell as every other image; only the panels and modules differ. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
```

### FIT-treehouse -- Treehouse seed

THE MODEL -- A 2V GEODESIC STEM-CELL DOME. The geometry below is exact; follow it rather than any general idea of a geodesic dome. (ONE) OVERALL FORM AND SCALE: exactly half a sphere, twice as wide as it is tall, standing on a flat ten-sided base ring. Full size it is about nineteen and a half feet across and nine and a half feet tall, and each long member is about six feet long; a person standing beside it reaches a little over halfway up. The surface is made of forty FLAT triangles (bays), so its outline is a crisp faceted polygon, never a smooth curve. (TWO) WHERE EVERY TRIANGLE GOES, TOP TO BOTTOM: at the very top, five struts meet at one point, the apex, making a five-pointed star of five triangles -- the crown. Directly under the crown, five equilateral triangles hang point-down, one beneath each outer side of the crown. Around the lower wall stand five more five-pointed stars, evenly spaced, each centred a little below halfway down the wall on a point where five struts meet; the lowest triangle of each lower star rests its wide side on the base ring with its point up at the star's centre. Between each pair of neighbouring lower stars, one of five more equilateral triangles stands point-up on the base ring. So the whole dome is six stars of five triangles plus ten equilateral triangles, forty in all. Every other junction has six struts meeting (ten of them); the ten corners of the base ring have four. (THREE) ONLY TWO STRUT LENGTHS: the SHORT struts (thirty lines) are exactly the spokes of the six stars -- every spoke is short and nothing else is. The LONG struts (thirty-five lines) are everything else: the outline of each star, the sides of the equilateral triangles, and all ten sides of the base ring. Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.

### FIT-workshop -- Workshop seed

*Use:* Carousel 'one frame, every building', Kickstarter gallery, web app · *Ratio:* 4:5 · *Style:* brick

**Prompt**

```
THE MODEL -- A 2V GEODESIC STEM-CELL DOME. The geometry below is exact; follow it rather than any general idea of a geodesic dome. (ONE) OVERALL FORM AND SCALE: exactly half a sphere, twice as wide as it is tall, standing on a flat ten-sided base ring. Full size it is about nineteen and a half feet across and nine and a half feet tall, and each long member is about six feet long; a person standing beside it reaches a little over halfway up. The surface is made of forty FLAT triangles (bays), so its outline is a crisp faceted polygon, never a smooth curve. (TWO) WHERE EVERY TRIANGLE GOES, TOP TO BOTTOM: at the very top, five struts meet at one point, the apex, making a five-pointed star of five triangles -- the crown. Directly under the crown, five equilateral triangles hang point-down, one beneath each outer side of the crown. Around the lower wall stand five more five-pointed stars, evenly spaced, each centred a little below halfway down the wall on a point where five struts meet; the lowest triangle of each lower star rests its wide side on the base ring with its point up at the star's centre. Between each pair of neighbouring lower stars, one of five more equilateral triangles stands point-up on the base ring. So the whole dome is six stars of five triangles plus ten equilateral triangles, forty in all. Every other junction has six struts meeting (ten of them); the ten corners of the base ring have four. (THREE) ONLY TWO STRUT LENGTHS: the SHORT struts (thirty lines) are exactly the spokes of the six stars -- every spoke is short and nothing else is. The LONG struts (thirty-five lines) are everything else: the outline of each star, the sides of the equilateral triangles, and all ten sides of the base ring. Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. THIS FIT-OUT: the workshop seed, standing as a plain hemisphere, with six skylight panels, four louvre panels, three window panels, one door panel, four solar panel bays, and plain closed panels in the other bays. SCENE: the dome in a rural toy yard, skylights bright in the upper ring, a workbench visible through the door, solar arrays on the sunward face. The frame is the same stem cell as every other image; only the panels and modules differ. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
```

**Overlay:** NURSERY SEED -- A room that attaches to the house rather than replacing it, and then becomes something else when it is grown out of. Which is the whole argument in one building.

### FIT-jacuzzi -- Jacuzzi seed

*Use:* Carousel 'one frame, every building', Kickstarter gallery, web app · *Ratio:* 4:5 · *Style:* brick

**Prompt**

```
THE MODEL -- A 2V GEODESIC STEM-CELL DOME. The geometry below is exact; follow it rather than any general idea of a geodesic dome. (ONE) OVERALL FORM AND SCALE: exactly half a sphere, twice as wide as it is tall, standing on a flat ten-sided base ring. Full size it is about nineteen and a half feet across and nine and a half feet tall, and each long member is about six feet long; a person standing beside it reaches a little over halfway up. The surface is made of forty FLAT triangles (bays), so its outline is a crisp faceted polygon, never a smooth curve. (TWO) WHERE EVERY TRIANGLE GOES, TOP TO BOTTOM: at the very top, five struts meet at one point, the apex, making a five-pointed star of five triangles -- the crown. Directly under the crown, five equilateral triangles hang point-down, one beneath each outer side of the crown. Around the lower wall stand five more five-pointed stars, evenly spaced, each centred a little below halfway down the wall on a point where five struts meet; the lowest triangle of each lower star rests its wide side on the base ring with its point up at the star's centre. Between each pair of neighbouring lower stars, one of five more equilateral triangles stands point-up on the base ring. So the whole dome is six stars of five triangles plus ten equilateral triangles, forty in all. Every other junction has six struts meeting (ten of them); the ten corners of the base ring have four. (THREE) ONLY TWO STRUT LENGTHS: the SHORT struts (thirty lines) are exactly the spokes of the six stars -- every spoke is short and nothing else is. The LONG struts (thirty-five lines) are everything else: the outline of each star, the sides of the equilateral triangles, and all ten sides of the base ring. Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. THIS FIT-OUT: the jacuzzi seed, turned over, with plain closed panels in every bay but the door. SCENE: the dome turned upside down and set into a deck as a round hot tub, the timber frame as its cradle, water steaming at night. The frame is the same stem cell as every other image; only the panels and modules differ. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
```

**Overlay:** JACUZZI SEED -- The dome turned over and used as the vessel. The shell becomes the tub and the frame becomes its cradle.

COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
```

**Overlay:** Utility hub: three iterations to a design that ships -- $9,500. The centre column is the one part that has to be right, because it carries every service and it is the part an owner cannot make. Three builds: one to find what breaks, one to fix it, one to prove the fix. Tooling, fittings, test rig and the parts thrown away.

**Prompt**

```
THE MODEL -- A 2V GEODESIC STEM-CELL DOME. The geometry below is exact; follow it rather than any general idea of a geodesic dome. (ONE) OVERALL FORM AND SCALE: exactly half a sphere, twice as wide as it is tall, standing on a flat ten-sided base ring. Full size it is about nineteen and a half feet across and nine and a half feet tall, and each long member is about six feet long; a person standing beside it reaches a little over halfway up. The surface is made of forty FLAT triangles (bays), so its outline is a crisp faceted polygon, never a smooth curve. (TWO) WHERE EVERY TRIANGLE GOES, TOP TO BOTTOM: at the very top, five struts meet at one point, the apex, making a five-pointed star of five triangles -- the crown. Directly under the crown, five equilateral triangles hang point-down, one beneath each outer side of the crown. Around the lower wall stand five more five-pointed stars, evenly spaced, each centred a little below halfway down the wall on a point where five struts meet; the lowest triangle of each lower star rests its wide side on the base ring with its point up at the star's centre. Between each pair of neighbouring lower stars, one of five more equilateral triangles stands point-up on the base ring. So the whole dome is six stars of five triangles plus ten equilateral triangles, forty in all. Every other junction has six struts meeting (ten of them); the ten corners of the base ring have four. (THREE) ONLY TWO STRUT LENGTHS: the SHORT struts (thirty lines) are exactly the spokes of the six stars -- every spoke is short and nothing else is. The LONG struts (thirty-five lines) are everything else: the outline of each star, the sides of the equilateral triangles, and all ten sides of the base ring. Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. SCENE: the dome as a dark faceted silhouette against a pale dawn gradient, only the rim of each flat panel catching light. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
```

**Overlay:** Something is growing. Follow for launch day.

STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. ### SOC-6 -- Milestone -- three quarters

*Use:* Instagram / TikTok / Facebook / X launch series · *Ratio:* 1:1 · *Style:* brick

**Prompt**

```
THE MODEL -- A 2V GEODESIC STEM-CELL DOME. The geometry below is exact; follow it rather than any general idea of a geodesic dome. (ONE) OVERALL FORM AND SCALE: exactly half a sphere, twice as wide as it is tall, standing on a flat ten-sided base ring. Full size it is about nineteen and a half feet across and nine and a half feet tall, and each long member is about six feet long; a person standing beside it reaches a little over halfway up. The surface is made of forty FLAT triangles (bays), so its outline is a crisp faceted polygon, never a smooth curve. (TWO) WHERE EVERY TRIANGLE GOES, TOP TO BOTTOM: at the very top, five struts meet at one point, the apex, making a five-pointed star of five triangles -- the crown. Directly under the crown, five equilateral triangles hang point-down, one beneath each outer side of the crown. Around the lower wall stand five more five-pointed stars, evenly spaced, each centred a little below halfway down the wall on a point where five struts meet; the lowest triangle of each lower star rests its wide side on the base ring with its point up at the star's centre. Between each pair of neighbouring lower stars, one of five more equilateral triangles stands point-up on the base ring. So the whole dome is six stars of five triangles plus ten equilateral triangles, forty in all. Every other junction has six struts meeting (ten of them); the ten corners of the base ring have four. (THREE) ONLY TWO STRUT LENGTHS: the SHORT struts (thirty lines) are exactly the spokes of the six stars -- every spoke is short and nothing else is. The LONG struts (thirty-five lines) are everything else: the outline of each star, the sides of the equilateral triangles, and all ten sides of the base ring. Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. SCENE: the same frame with three quarters of its bays panelled. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.

(TWO) WHERE EVERY TRIANGLE GOES, TOP TO BOTTOM: at the very top, five struts meet at one point, the apex, making a five-pointed star of five triangles -- the crown. Directly under the crown, five equilateral triangles hang point-down, one beneath each outer side of the crown. Around the lower wall stand five more five-pointed stars, evenly spaced, each centred a little below halfway down the wall on a point where five struts meet; the lowest triangle of each lower star rests its wide side on the base ring with its point up at the star's centre. Between each pair of neighbouring lower stars, one of five more equilateral triangles stands point-up on the base ring. So the whole dome is six stars of five triangles plus ten equilateral triangles, forty in all. Every other junction has six struts meeting (ten of them); the ten corners of the base ring have four. (THREE) ONLY TWO STRUT LENGTHS: the SHORT struts (thirty lines) are exactly the spokes of the six stars -- every spoke is short and nothing else is. The LONG struts (thirty-five lines) are everything else: the outline of each star, the sides of the equilateral triangles, and all ten sides of the base ring. Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. SCENE: Lumen, the project's mascot: a small low-polygon jellyfish, faceted like the dome, with a softly glowing translucent cyan bell and a few trailing faceted tentacles; friendly and curious, never goofy. Small in frame, never covering the dome. Lumen floats beside the dome, one tentacle pointing at a seam as if about to explain it. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
```

**Overlay:** Lumen says: the seam is a channel. Ask why in the comments.

Between each pair of neighbouring lower stars, one of five more equilateral triangles stands point-up on the base ring. So the whole dome is six stars of five triangles plus ten equilateral triangles, forty in all. Every other junction has six struts meeting (ten of them); the ten corners of the base ring have four. (THREE) ONLY TWO STRUT LENGTHS: the SHORT struts (thirty lines) are exactly the spokes of the six stars -- every spoke is short and nothing else is. The LONG struts (thirty-five lines) are everything else: the outline of each star, the sides of the equilateral triangles, and all ten sides of the base ring. Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. SCENE: the dome in the left third, clean sky on the right for a large headline. High contrast, readable small. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
```

**Overlay:** Headline set in type: three to five words, e.g. 'WHY WEDGES?'

Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. SCENE: the dome small in the lower third at blue hour, a tall empty indigo gradient above it for a column of buttons. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). CAMERA: box-art three-quarter view from about thirty degrees above the ground, slight telephoto so straight lines stay straight, the model centred with generous margins. No text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no brand names, no box or packaging, no watermark -- only the model. COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
```

### MOTION-4 -- The core lights up

*Use:* Veo / Gemini video: Reels, TikTok, Shorts loops, GIFs · *Ratio:* 9:16, six to eight seconds · *Style:* brick

**Prompt**

```
THE MODEL -- A 2V GEODESIC STEM-CELL DOME. The geometry below is exact; follow it rather than any general idea of a geodesic dome. (ONE) OVERALL FORM AND SCALE: exactly half a sphere, twice as wide as it is tall, standing on a flat ten-sided base ring. Full size it is about nineteen and a half feet across and nine and a half feet tall, and each long member is about six feet long; a person standing beside it reaches a little over halfway up. The surface is made of forty FLAT triangles (bays), so its outline is a crisp faceted polygon, never a smooth curve. (TWO) WHERE EVERY TRIANGLE GOES, TOP TO BOTTOM: at the very top, five struts meet at one point, the apex, making a five-pointed star of five triangles -- the crown. Directly under the crown, five equilateral triangles hang point-down, one beneath each outer side of the crown. Around the lower wall stand five more five-pointed stars, evenly spaced, each centred a little below halfway down the wall on a point where five struts meet; the lowest triangle of each lower star rests its wide side on the base ring with its point up at the star's centre. Between each pair of neighbouring lower stars, one of five more equilateral triangles stands point-up on the base ring. So the whole dome is six stars of five triangles plus ten equilateral triangles, forty in all. Every other junction has six struts meeting (ten of them); the ten corners of the base ring have four. (THREE) ONLY TWO STRUT LENGTHS: the SHORT struts (thirty lines) are exactly the spokes of the six stars -- every spoke is short and nothing else is. The LONG struts (thirty-five lines) are everything else: the outline of each star, the sides of the equilateral triangles, and all ten sides of the base ring. Long is only about one-eighth longer than short, so every triangle looks nearly equilateral; the difference is subtle but consistent, and the two-tone colour key makes it readable. (FOUR) EVERY MEMBER IS A WEDGE -- POINT IN, FLAT FACE OUT: a solid, straight timber bar whose cross-section is a TRIANGLE, like one slice of a pie split from a round log, about four and a half inches across its flat face and six inches deep from that face to its point. It lies with the FLAT FACE ON THE OUTSIDE of the dome, flush with the surface, and the SHARP POINT AIMED INWARD at the centre of the dome. From outside you see flat faces; from inside you see a lattice of sharp ridges. Each bay has ITS OWN three wedges forming a triangular frame, so along every line between two bays lie TWO wedges side by side, back to back (fifty-five doubled seams), while each of the ten base-ring sides has a single wedge -- one hundred and twenty wedges in all. The angled sides of two neighbouring wedges do not close flat against each other: along every seam they leave a slim V-shaped channel, open to the inside, running unbroken from junction to junction. At each corner of a bay the three wedges lap pinwheel-fashion, each end butting against the side of the next rather than being cut to a point. There are NO hubs, plates, bolts or connectors anywhere: wedges meet wedges directly. (FIVE) PANELS: each bay is closed by one flat triangular panel laid ON the outside face of its own three wedges and pulled tight by four small flush screw heads, so the outside of a closed dome is a continuous faceted skin of panels with thin, even seam lines between them. Behind every panel the depth of the wedges leaves an empty triangular cavity; the seam channels connect all of these cavities. (SIX) THE CENTRE: the apex has the only opening in the whole surface, closed by a round cyan seal cap -- a lid with small over-centre catches around its rim. Directly beneath it the utility column, a slim square cyan upright chase, rises from the centre of the floor to the apex. (SEVEN) THE BASE: the ten corners of the base ring rest on the pad, a flat ten-sided timber platform a little wider than the dome, with one square service port at its exact centre, directly under the column. MOTION: Cutaway model: the power line glows yellow from the pedestal up the column to the light at the apex, then the water line glows blue from the tank to the manifold, then the drain grey back down. AVOID: metal hubs or connector plates, round dowel or pipe struts, square-cornered dimensional lumber, a full sphere, a smooth curved dome, more or fewer than the stated stars, a tent or canvas skin, party lights, rainbow colours outside the colour key, confetti, glue, patches, mismatched panels, weathered wood, studs on the dome, garbled text, any words or numbers, logos, watermarks. STYLE -- A PREMIUM BUILDING-BRICK MODEL SET. Render the subject as a top-quality construction-toy model photographed for the front of its box. Every part is a distinct, separately moulded piece: perfectly crisp edges, tiny uniform bevels on every corner, smooth satin plastic surfaces, bright clean colours, no wear, no dust, no weathering. Parts look as if they click together: joints are exact, the gaps between pieces are thin even shadow lines, and nothing is glued, taped, bent, patched or improvised. Proportions are TRUE to the real building -- a scale model, not a cartoon: no squashing, no oversized parts, no rounded blobby shapes. The only studs (round bumps) in the picture are on the green ground baseplate and along the rim of the pad; the dome itself has none. Scale people, where named, are simple blocky unbranded toy figures with cylindrical heads and a friendly painted face, standing a little over half the dome's height. LIGHT: bright soft studio light from the upper left, gentle shadow in every joint, soft contact shadows on the baseplate, a faint glossy highlight on the panels. BACKGROUND: a clean seamless gradient, pale sky-blue fading to white, unless a scene is named; a named scene is built from toy parts too (studded green baseplates, brick-built pine trees with stacked cone foliage, round tile paths). COLOUR KEY -- identical in every image of this series, so a part is recognisable from one picture to the next: LONG wedge members = warm natural tan with a faint printed wood grain; SHORT wedge members = a deeper honey-caramel tan, so the short members visibly draw the six five-pointed stars across the dome; plain panels = matte cream white; window and skylight panels = clear pale-blue translucent; door panel = charcoal grey; the DOME OWNER's hardware (utility column, apex seal cap, utility panels, the dome's own floor) = bright cyan-teal; the PAD HOST's hardware (service port, electrical pedestal, frost valve, water tank, under-floor hatches) = bright warm amber (hex FFB13E); service lines = power bright yellow, water blue, drain dark grey, air white; the pad's deck = light brown plank tiles on dark grey pier blocks; the ground = a green studded baseplate.
